Phase 3.40 needs your decision before I brief the planner: whether it runs, and how big it is.

The generation plan left this open until 3.39's leftovers (the "residue") were counted. Phase 3.39 closed by recording about 292,000 cast sites as residue instead of fixing them. These are places in the C code that read struct fields through raw pointer arithmetic, and they stay that way because their structs have no single agreed definition yet. That is roughly 13,000 struct types, and the top 81 cover half the sites. The lever residue file also lists 2,433 rows covering 3,528 sites. A "lever" here is a pin or inline asm we use to steer the compiler, and 70 of those rows are tagged as needing an agent.

My recommendation is to run it in full. Giving those structs canonical definitions and getting the lever check to a true zero is the real goal of this generation. Phase 3.39 only covered these sites with records, and the naming phases (3.42–3.43) work much better on real struct members. Starting with the top 81 types keeps it manageable.

## 214005-016r-claude-answer
---
ts: 2026-10-15T03:40:05.361Z

usage: {input: 8, cacheRead: 136845, cacheWrite: 4537, output: 2221}
---

The repo is a PC port built on top of our project, not a rival decomp. Its credits name "Druthulu (Drew T), author of Druthulu/BFM-decomp", and I checked that this is our own `origin` remote.

**What it is.** It's an experimental PC port of the US release (SLUS-00726), about two weeks old: first commit 2026-09-30, 7 commits, a Linux release r38 and a Windows test build r41.
- It runs mostly as a hybrid. A CPU interpreter runs the game's original code from your own disc, and compiled native code replaces pieces as they get decompiled.
- It uses SDL2, OpenAL and OpenGL, and draws through PsyCross, an open-source library that reimplements the PlayStation's graphics on a PC.
- It has about 252 hand-matched C functions of its own, all in `main`. Attract mode stops at one unsupported function, and its next goal is a playable Chapter 1 with saving.
- It says it contains none of our code. It does take our splat layout (how the game's binaries are cut into files) and our symbol names for its config, and it checks its own C against ours so it can leave out anything derived from us.

**What we'd gain.** Not much now, nothing that helps 3.40 to 3.45. We have every function matched and are already past the stage it is at. The one useful thing is its architecture doc. It shows a clean "platform seam": one interface the game calls for graphics, audio, input and saves, with a backend behind each. That's worth reading when the next generation starts the shiftable build (a build where code can move in memory and still work), since a port is a natural next step after that.

**What they'd gain.** A lot. They lack typed structs and real names, and they cover only 252 functions where we have all of them. As our 3.40 to 3.43 work lands, they can take whole subsystems from our C instead of matching them again, and our struct types would make their native code readable.

**One thing to check.** Our `tools/` and `docs/` are AGPL-3.0 and our `src/` has no license. Their port is MIT, and part of their config was "derived from the upstream BFM-decomp splat layout." That is probably fine: splat layouts and symbol lists are mostly facts, and they credit us properly. But whether you're happy with MIT reuse of what you've published is your call. It's also a reason to give `src/` an explicit license someday.

**My recommendation:** send Blizz127 a friendly note. Thank them for the credit, point them to our struct and naming phases as the thing that would help them most, and ask them to keep the attribution. Don't change our plan for this. I can save the port's architecture doc as a pointer for the next generation's port or shiftable-build planning, and draft the note if you want one.

Tell me how you'd like to handle it, or if you want to dig into anything first. When you're ready, I'll brief the planner for 3.40 in full, as you chose.

To resolve it, I need to define canonical types for the map types, merge the hash-named twins duplicated across overlay families, then rerun rung S over those merged bodies to fix the cast-to-member translation.

Each rung-S batch gets gated by R22 and committed through the restruct cycle script. Looking at the sizing, full TNC elimination spans nearly 292,000 sites across 13,000 types, with 95% coverage needing roughly 4,400 types — the long tail of single-site types is what makes defining canonical headers for all 13,000 such a large undertaking, especially since each canonical definition would need to come from head_types generating struct layouts from field-offset data. I'm trying to pin down whether this generation process is mechanical, and how folding duplicate twin types onto a single canonical type factors into reducing that residue.

Other TNC causes like scalar-extern and width mismatches get bucketed separately — the 3.40 goal is specifically to shrink residue through genuine fixes while leaving everything else properly marked with pass/instrument/cause tags, so I'm narrowing focus to TNC and lever fixes plus any opportunistic NO-FIELD cleanup already decided in 3.38. For the lever strict-zero target, driving it to literal zero means replacing all 1,868 oracle-judged-necessary register pins (1,496 of them asm) with C-level levers that reproduce the same codegen without relying on pinning — essentially substituting cookbook-style levers like allocno class control for what the pins currently enforce.

## 215107-020r-claude-answer
---
ts: 2026-10-15T03:51:07.469Z
session: a4742bc1-12ac-4649-ab77-694e7315677f
agentId: a1ce918197a39b8ec
model: claude-opus-5-5[1m]
effort: medium
turnId: 6fc7430e-a08e-4f8d-bfd0-045c00b69643
usage: {input: 4, cacheRead: 51289, cacheWrite: 664, output: 997}
---

Most likely there's no issue. The question is only whether anything they copied from us was AGPL-covered.

**MIT (theirs)** is a "do what you like" license. Anyone can copy, change, sell or fold the code into closed-source software, as long as they keep the copyright notice. It asks nothing back.

**AGPL-3.0 (our `tools/` and `docs/`)** is a "share-alike" license, and the strictest common one. Anyone may use and change our tools, but anything they build from them and distribute must also be AGPL, with full source. The "A" adds that this applies even when the software only runs on a server for others to use. The point is that improvements to our work stay open.

**Where it could clash:** if they had copied one of our AGPL tools or docs and released it as MIT, that would break our license, because AGPL code can't be relicensed as permissive. From what the retriever saw, they haven't done that. They say they contain none of our code, and what they took is the splat layout and symbol names. Those are essentially facts about the game's binary (addresses, sizes, labels) and live in our config, not in `tools/` or `docs/`. Their own tools look self-written. So I see no actual conflict, though I haven't checked their files line by line.

**The one real point is our `src/`, which has no license.** With no license, the default is "all rights reserved", so strictly nobody may reuse our decompiled C. (The C also comes from Square's game, so whose rights cover it is murky anyway, which is common for decomp projects.) That matters later: if they want our typed structs and named code in 3.43 and beyond, they technically have no permission to take them.

My recommendation is to do nothing now. When you want others to build on the legible source, put an explicit license on `src/`. Pick a permissive one like MIT or CC0 if you want ports to use it freely, or AGPL if you want their changes to stay open. That's your call and not urgent. This is a plain reading of the licenses, not legal advice.

The planner is still drafting 3.40. I'll bring you the draft when it's ready.
## 215107-094w-claude-thinking
---
ts: 2026-10-15T03:51:07.397Z

effort: medium
turnId: 6fc7430e-a08e-4f8d-bfd0-045c00b69643
---
I should give a plain, direct answer: MIT is permissive and allows closed-source use as long as the notice is kept, while AGPL-3.0 is copyleft and requires derivatives—including network-run services—to stay AGPL with source shared. If their code derives from AGPL-licensed tools or docs, relicensing it as MIT wouldn't be allowed; and since our src/ carries no license, it defaults to all rights reserved, complicated further by the fact it's decompiled code from a copyrighted game.

They claim they copied none of our actual code, only the memory layout and symbol names, which are likely uncopyrightable facts rather than protected expression, and those live in config rather than the tools. So this probably isn't an issue unless they lifted actual AGPL tool code — their own tooling seems independently written. The unlicensed src also cuts both ways, technically restricting anyone from copying it, which is worth considering if we want to make the source more permissively reusable going forward.

One correction first: AGPL doesn't stop anyone selling. No standard open-source license does, and MIT, GPL and AGPL all allow selling. AGPL only forces anyone who sells a derived work to release its full source under AGPL too. To rule out selling you need a **non-commercial** license, which by the usual definition is "source-available" rather than open source.

**What fits what you want** ("free community mods fine, selling not"):
- **CC BY-NC-SA 4.0** is the best-known one. Reuse is free with credit, nothing commercial is allowed, and derivatives must use the same terms. It's widely used for docs and data, and some decomp projects use it for source.
- **PolyForm Noncommercial 1.0** is written for software and bans commercial use. It doesn't force derivatives to stay under the same terms.

My recommendation is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 for `src/` and `docs/`, the project's real value. Leave `tools/` on AGPL unless you also mind someone selling the tooling itself. The vendored `tools/brave-CUE/` is someone else's GPL code and can't be relicensed.

**Limits to keep in mind:**
- **Their own code stays theirs.** You can't change the MIT on code Blizz127 wrote. A new license only covers what you publish, and only from now on.
- **Facts aren't covered.** Addresses and splat layouts probably aren't protected by any license.
- **Selling is already blocked.** A paid BFM port would infringe Square Enix's rights to the game no matter what our license says. A non-commercial license mainly makes your intent explicit and covers future reuse of our structs and names.
- **You need to be the copyright holder.** You can relicense only if you are the only human contributor. Anything someone else contributed needs their consent.

If you want to go ahead, I'd make it a small task of its own, outside 3.40, so the plan stays as it is: add a `LICENSE` for `src/` and `docs/`, update the README's license section and the existing AGPL headers, and run `audit_public.py`. I can do that alongside the planner if you confirm the license choice and that all contributions are yours.

I should correct the misconception that AGPL forbids selling — no OSI license actually does. What's needed instead is a non-commercial license like CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 or PolyForm Noncommercial, though they can't relicense code that's already theirs under MIT, nor retroactively restrict facts already taken. Practically, selling a BFM port is Square Enix's IP problem regardless.

This is a licensing preference, not a decided fact, so I shouldn't record it on the developer card yet — it's near the size cap anyway. ## Rationale
- Types before levers: rung S re-sweeps respell thousands of bodies; lever cracks after that run on the final body text, so no crack is redone. Types may also explain pins (the 3.39 premise).
- The canonical-types campaign is mechanical and tool-driven (write-types, rung S, residue rewrite), comparable to 3.39 T3+T4. It is pilot-gated: T2 measures conversion rate, header fan-out cost and new causes on the top 75 types (50% of TNC) before T3-T5 fan out. Planner judgment: TNC 0 is realistic in one phase if T2's pilot converts at T3's rate. T2 returns `blocked` with a projection if it does not.
- The literal lever 0 is agent-priced. About 800 crack targets remain after the cheap tools ran dry (3.39 T8: 0 pins removed by delever R/E/G). The comparable legacy P36 lane took 9 sessions and about 15-20M tokens. The plan goes class by class, cheapest real fix first:
  - T8: re-judge 1,013 not-judged/author-left rows with the oracle; the GTE, asm-macro and asm-body classes;
  - T7: missing-parameter pins become declared parameters, a signature fix through rung D, as with 3.39 T6 lying decls;
  - T9: head-class cracks, where one crack propagates to every copy;
  - T10: a singleton pilot that measures crack rate and cost;
  - T11: the singleton wave at the developer's cap.
  T10 is the checkpoint: if its projection exceeds the cap, it returns `blocked` with numbers and Claude puts the milestone to the developer (see Developer decides).
- T2 is `effort: high`. Its judgment: the fold policy for map types, meaning when two hash-named layouts are one entity (fold onto one canonical) and when they are coincident (separate types). Also which canonical name and file each type gets. Bytes cannot arbitrate type identity. 1 high task in 14.
- Legacy-T7 items (T6) come before the missing-parameter bulk (T7): the func_801A1E94 pair is a missing-parameter cascade, and its fix is the template for T7.
- T12 (timeline uncovered series) runs right after T1 so every later snapshot draws it. T14 carries the standing phase-close docs request (developer 2026-10-02).
- Every task that respells bodies leaves the per-site cast gate red (C0528). Each such task re-runs `restruct --rung S --residue` for its segments before it is done, and T13 re-runs it fleet-wide.

## Cookbook
Types: C0511 (type-census pitfalls, stale ledger rows), C0512 (fold duplicate struct defs onto one canonical type), C0520 (hash-named bbt types block a rung-S redraw), C0521 (match KEPT on a normalised base), C0522 (dead-kept canonical types go live), C0523 (`--plan` skips .h unless `--headers`), C0524 (an 'unsigned' class may mean 'cache never saw it'), C0526 (read a gate's predicate before planning to zero it), C0528 (residue per site; re-run after respelling), C0507 (§458 S1/S2 ladder).
Declarations: C0516 (lying decls: def + all callers; K&R-empty can be load-bearing), C0503 (rungs D/R).
Levers: C0505 (lever-removal move catalog), C0517 (GTE one spelling; clobber variants as header levers), C0527 (rung R no-op brace wraps), C0087 (§76 allocno class via declaration scope), C0119 (§108 diagnosing a family 0/N), C0149 (§138 propagation lanes by blast radius), C0287 (§266 inert-rider law), C0278 (§257 dead-end ledger).
Docs: C0518 (freshness checks mask self-referential cells), C0514 (repo-wide check on unedited HEAD first; kit-corpus).
Rules: R22, R42, R53, R99, R100, R102, R110, R113, R124, R126, R127 (a tool change ships with wiring and docs), R128 (phase records keep the narrative axis).

## Research
R3.40-001 (lever_residue.tsv and lever_census flags/predicates/writer), R3.40-002 (TNC predicate, canon types, residue_census shape, restruct rungs/ledger, timeline inputs), R3.40-003 (PhaseEnd 3.39 decisions/carry/recap, T12 doc paths, legacy-T7 item evidence), R3.39-001 (3.39 cast/lever/lying instruments), R3.38-002 (type_census tiers, dead_canon, check-structs, lift_types scope). Planner counts not in a report: residue_census `tnc` and top types, the lever_residue breakdown (`.run/planner340/lever_counts.py`), the live `lever_census --check --strict [--residue]` line, timeline.py/readability_progress.py lines above.

## Developer decides
- Lever finish line and agent cap. As decided, the milestone keeps the literal `lever_census --check --strict` 0, which is also a Generation-3 done-criterion. Planner sizing: about 800 crack targets after the cheap tools ran dry (438 singleton functions, 38 head classes, 270 missing-parameter functions, 9 GTE, 11 asm macros, 13 asm bodies). No cap is named, and the standing constraint runs agent waves only at the developer's cap. Recommendation: name a cap now (4 concurrent crack coders), keep the literal 0, and accept that T10 stops the wave with a cost projection if the singleton tail projects past that cap. Claude then puts the fallback milestone wording (in the planner's report) to the developer with T10's numbers.
- Ledger durability (D1 open items). Drafted in T1: untrack `.run/P36/delever/ledger.jsonl` (history keeps its 81 MB blob, below the limit), and keep gzip snapshots of both ledgers in `.run/keep/` (untracked) at phase start and in T13. Recommendation: approve as drafted and keep an off-repo copy of `.run/keep/` yourself. The pre-commit size guard is harness work and goes to the ProjectArchitect repo as a `harness:` gotcha.

- P3.39-1: T14 -- the standing phase-close docs request (README graphs, story §10, retrospective §7, wiki), as P3.39 T12 did

## Risks
- The TNC count may partly be stale cause stamps (the 80126B58 anomaly): then TNC falls by a ledger rewrite with no respell, which is not a real fix. T1/T2 must separate the two, and the record counts MEMBER/KEPT conversions, not only the by-cause drop.
- Writing about 13k canonical types may conflict with `type_census --check` 0 dup defs / 0 dead names (twins, single-site types with no declared user). The T2 policy must keep both at 0 at T5's end.
- Rung-S re-sweep cost: header fan-outs (about 6 min each) and about 38k TNC bodies. If T2's projection exceeds the phase, T2 returns blocked rather than narrowing.
- The literal lever 0 is agent-priced and open-ended (P36: 9 sessions). T10 is the stop that turns an overrun into a developer ruling, never a silent narrowing.
- Lever cracks and rung D signature edits respell bodies, so per-site cast residue goes red after each (C0528): re-run `--residue` before every task's verify.
- Asm-body routines moved to assembly units could read as leaving the census. Only evidence that the original was hand-written assembly justifies it.
- 3.39 numbers differ by tree (T10 vs T9 TNC figures; P37 vs P39 census dirs by about 5 sites). Baselines come only from T1's census on the start commit.

**Health and gates**
- R22 is 218/218 on fae956dbcb.
- `make tools-health` first failed. The PA 3.0 install added `tools/journal_rollup.py` without a tool-dictionary row and removed `tools/expert_ttl.py` while its row stayed, which left the kit corpus stale. I swapped the row, ran `make kit-corpus`, and tools-health now passes.
- delever_oracle `--snapshot-baseline` and `--calibrate ov_SC04_011 ov_SC03_015 md_SC07_004 main -j 16` are green: 146/146 identical, and the positive control differs as it should.
- `restruct --selftest --real` is 114/114.

**Census dirs**
- Settled: the gate, restruct and the cycle read `.run/P37/census`. The readability series reads the newest `.run/P<N>/census` (P39 today). The lever census reads `.run/P36/census`.
- Run on the same tree, P37, P39 and a no-cache run give identical counts, so the folders never disagreed.
- The ~5-site gap is two predicates, not two dirs. The gate drops 6 cast sites that sit on `#define` lines in `include/common.h` (5 P, 1 M; type_census.py:2039). The series counts them. The README showed both numbers side by side.
- No tooling change was needed.

**Unkstruct_80126B58**
- It is a stale cause stamp, not a name mismatch: the type is canonical.
- The planner's 5,482 is its count of covered sites across all causes. Only 3,457 of those carry TYPE-NOT-CANONICAL, and the live cause for them is now NO-EDIT or TYPE-NOT-VISIBLE.
- It is the only stale-stamped type in the fleet, so live TNC is 288,362 rather than the stamped 291,819.

**Milestone instruments**
I read each predicate and ran 14 planted controls; all 14 behaved as predicted.
- Two parts fail today, as the plan expects: check-casts, through its TNC and lying counts (291,819 and 11), and the literal `lever_census --check --strict` (rc 1).
- Four already pass on today's tree: `restruct --check-residue` (0 uncovered in every segment, every form), `--check-structs` (OK), `lever_census --check` (0 UNMARKED) and R22. They cannot be shown failing on today's counts, so I showed each failing on a planted control instead. I recorded this under Deviations in the summary.

**Ledgers**
- `.run/P36/delever/ledger.jsonl` is untracked and `.gitignore` names both ledgers.
- Gz snapshots of both ledgers are in `.run/keep/`, checksums verified.

Recommendation: treat the four already-passing checks as guards. This phase's progress shows only in the TNC count, the lying count and the literal lever gate. T2 should re-derive TNC from the live cause before trusting any count, because the stamped cause can be cleared by re-stamping without a real fix.

For clusters confined to unedited bodies, though, the union order is likely stable since their relative sequence doesn't change, so their roots should stay consistent. No, we commit only to the public repo. The archive is a one-time snapshot, and nothing goes to it anymore.

**What the archive is for.** `Druthulu/BFM-decomp-archive` is a private repo you made on 2026-09-06, just before taking the project public. Until then the repo was private and held things that can't be published: the Ghidra project, RAM dumps, Sony's PsyQ SDK, extension zips and the old session archive. Those were purged from the public history before the switch to public on 2026-09-08. The archive keeps that private-era state as a backup of those binaries. Since then they are backed up as text instead: the Ghidra work as `config/ghidra/*.jsonl`, rebuildable with `tools/ghidra_rebuild.sh --proof`, plus checksums for the dumps and the SDK. The background is in `/home/musashi/bfm-decomp/docs/ops/backup-repository-posture-rules-r20r21-public-since-2026-09-08-phase-34.md`.

**Where the Claude material lives.** It's all in the public repo. This repo has a single remote, `origin` = `Druthulu/BFM-decomp`, and about 255 tracked files in `.claude/`, `phase-ends/` and `research/` alone. On top of those, the rules, the cookbook, the card (`HOW_WE_WORK.md`) and the journal are also committed there. Every task commit lands there once you push. What keeps proprietary bytes out is `tools/audit_public.py` and a CI check.

If you'd rather keep the agent machinery (plans, logs, journal, agent definitions) out of the public repo, that's a bigger change. It would need a second private repo or a gitignore split, and the PA tools expect those files to be tracked. My recommendation is to leave it as it is: none of it is secret, and it documents how the decomp was made. If you want to change it, I can add it to the inbox like the license item.

This doesn't touch the plan, so I've let the queue continue. T2, the canonical-types pilot, is running.

Understood, the Claude material stays in the public repo.

**`.run/` is mostly scratch, but not all of it.** It holds about 40 GB, and `.gitignore` ignores it by default (`/.run/*`). A few folders are deliberately exempted, about 6,700 tracked files, mainly from Phases 32 to 37: agent crack records, harnesses and conversion ledgers. The rule written in `.gitignore` is "commit what a rerun cannot reproduce; leave anything a script regenerates ignored." Those exempted files are hand or agent analysis that would cost real compute to redo, so they're in git on purpose. They're small text and contain nothing proprietary.

**Disaster recovery depends on two large files that are now only on this machine:**
- `.run/P37/restruct/ledger.jsonl` (296 MB) records every struct-rewrite decision. The residue coverage check that closed 3.39 reads it.
- `.run/P36/delever/ledger.jsonl` (81 MB) records the lever-removal attempts.

Neither can live on GitHub: one is over the 100 MB limit and the other was close to it, which is why T1 untracked the second today.

If this disk died, the source would be safe: `src/` is in git, and every binary still byte-matches. What we'd lose is the record behind the coverage check and the "already tried this" memory. Rebuilding that would mean re-running a lot of agent and compile work.

T1 made compressed snapshots, `.run/keep/p37-restruct-ledger-20261014.jsonl.gz` (14 MB) and `.run/keep/p36-delever-ledger-20261014.jsonl.gz` (3 MB). Those are also only local, which is why I asked you to keep a copy elsewhere.

**My recommendation:** copy those two `.gz` files off this machine at each phase close. Because they're small, the simplest home is the private `BFM-decomp-archive` repo, or any cloud folder. If you like, I can inbox a small task for the phase close to commit them to the archive repo automatically. You'd still do the push.

This doesn't change the plan, so the queue is moving again.

I should set the pin as a merge of the current census bbt with the P39 pin, written once as a snapshot, so the earlier lineage is preserved even though the table remap handles the rename anyway. ---

They're almost entirely free of Square content, but not quite. That and repo growth are the two real reasons not to commit them as they are. I sampled every 50th record of both ledgers to check.

**What they contain.** Mostly bookkeeping: file paths, function names, hashes, verdicts ("needed", "residue"), compiler error messages and function signatures. The lever ledger also stores before and after copies of our C function bodies, which are no more sensitive than `src/`, already public.

**The catch is in the restruct ledger.** When a rewrite changed the compiled output, it saved the differing instruction pairs. These are lines like `1040005a beqz v0,2c14 <func_80180088+0x424>`: a raw machine-code word plus its disassembly. Because our build is byte-identical to the retail game, those words are Square's compiled code. Our standing public rule (H1) says no ROM-derived bytes in git, which is why `asm/` and `expected/` are never committed. The fragments are tiny, but the rule has no size exception, and `tools/audit_public.py` exists to enforce it.

**The second reason is size over time.** Git keeps every version of a file forever. The restruct ledger grows with each batch, so committing a 14 MB snapshot every phase adds about 14 MB of permanent history each time, and much more if it's committed per batch. The repo's whole design keeps big generated files out of history.

**My recommendation, in two parts:**
- At each phase close, commit a stripped copy of both ledgers to the public repo, compressed, with the machine-code field removed. That's small, legal and pushable, and it covers disaster recovery for the coverage check and the attempt history.
- Put the full, unstripped snapshots in the private archive repo, where H1 doesn't apply.

That's a small tool plus a phase-close step. If you agree, I'll put it in the inbox for this phase's records task (T13) so it starts this phase. Until then, a manual copy of the two `.gz` files is the backup.

None of this touches the running plan, so the queue is moving.

I've added a ledger backup task to Phase 3.40, set to run after T13 (the milestone gate). It lands at the next task boundary, so T2 isn't affected.

The task does four things:
- **New tool:** it adds `tools/ledger_backup.py`, which makes two compressed copies of each ledger at every phase close.
- **Public copy:** it removes every field that carries game machine code, checking every record rather than a sample. It commits the result to `docs/ledgers/` in this repo and must pass `audit_public.py`.
- **Archive copy:** the full, unstripped copy is committed to a local clone of your private `BFM-decomp-archive` repo, at `../BFM-decomp-archive` under `ledgers/P<phase>/`. The task clones the repo if needed. If the clone fails, for example on credentials, it stops and reports to me.
- **Restore check and routine:** it confirms each public copy unzips and has the right record count. It also documents the step and adds a pointer to the card, so every later phase close runs it.

One thing for you: nothing pushes either repo, so after the phase closes you'll push the archive repo as well as this one.

PA3 has received another fix release. The version number stays 3.28.1, and it adds three fixes to the two you saw earlier:
- In the activity band, the planner's name now shows in the planners' purple, matching its cards.
- A plan change you ask for during a phase goes in at the next task boundary, and I tell you in chat what changed. The plan view now opens only for a phase's first plan and for the generation plan. The ledger-backup task I just added is one of these changes.
- My questions appear the moment I ask them, even while a task runs, and answering one doesn't disturb that task.

Please restart Claude Code. This update changed a setting that only takes effect after a restart, plus the mod's own files, and no new task starts until the new mod loads. /reload-plugins would load the mod, but only a restart applies the setting.

Since bclass checks come first in the residue_cause_im ordering, those earlier TNC stamps were likely written pre-T10, so restamping to GADDR/GLOBAL reflects the correct live classifier logic. This restamping might extend beyond the top-75 scope into the long tail since drawn-type resolution could shift sites there too, which is honest but worth reporting separately from the top-75 before/after metrics.

Checking whether by-cause counts read the row verdict confirms they read entry causes instead, so TYPE-NOT-CANONICAL verdicts don't interfere there. The 8011F888 merge will push certain sites to NO-FIELD during the sweep.

For the sweep itself, I'm planning the sequence: pin the census snapshot with the P39 pin, commit the table edit first to keep a clean tree, run a plan check to count drawable files/bodies, then run the detached restruct cycle with the pin and target list across batches, committing each batch. Afterward I'll refresh the census, run residue checks across segments, then restamp and verify residue per segment before moving to the next coder batch for field pruning and struct checks.

I'm now thinking the next re-sweep would need to catch 8011F888 bodies, with a final residue/restamp/check pass — maybe c3 handles sweep plus residue rewrite while c4 does batch 2 and the final pass. I also want to account for the dirty tree state: modified census files, the generated struct-map doc, the discussion index, and journal entries, while confirming the cycle's clean-check only cares about src/ and config/. I need to clarify whether the cycle commits the ledger (it doesn't, since that's disabled) and which phase log actually receives the batch entry — the coder will need to check that. Since the cycle runs detached for hours, I'm considering how the coder should monitor it given tool calls are capped at 285 seconds each, likely via a watch skill.

The skill handles that monitoring, so I'll go ahead and commit the table edit first.
